=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
A sequence X = (X_1, X_2, \ldots) of positive integers (possibly empty) is called a 1122 sequence if and only if it satisfies all of the following three conditions: (The definition of a 1122 sequence is the same as in Problem F.)

- \lvert X \rvert is even. Here, \lvert X \rvert denotes the length of X.
- For each integer i satisfying 1\leq i\leq \frac{|X|}{2}, X_{2i-1} and X_{2i} are equal.
- Each positive integer appears in X either not at all or exactly twice. That is, every positive integer contained in X appears exactly twice in X.

Given a sequence A = (A_1, A_2, \ldots, A_N) of length N consisting of positive integers, print the maximum length of a (contiguous) subarray of A that is a 1122 sequence.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
A_1 A_2 \ldots A_N

Output

Print the maximum length of a (contiguous) subarray of A that is a 1122 sequence.

Constraints


- 1\leq N \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 1\leq A_i \leq N
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

8
2 3 1 1 2 2 1 1

Sample Output 1

4

For example, taking the subarray from the 3-rd to 6-th elements of A, we get (1, 1, 2, 2), which is a 1122 sequence of length 4.
There is no longer (contiguous) subarray that satisfies the conditions for a 1122 sequence, so the answer is 4.

Sample Input 2

3
1 2 2

Sample Output 2

2

Sample Input 3

1
1

Sample Output 3

0

Note that a sequence of length 0 also satisfies the conditions for a 1122 sequence.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.


Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Need understand definition: Sequence X positive integers (possibly empty) called 1122 sequence iff:
- length even
- for each i 1..|X|/2, X_{2i-1}=X_{2i}: pairs adjacent equal.
- Each positive integer appears either not at all or exactly twice. So sequence is concatenation of pairs of equal numbers, and each number appears in at most one pair. Thus a 1122 sequence is an even-length sequence where elements are arranged as adjacent equal pairs, and all pair values distinct.

Given A length N up to 2e5, Ai <= N. Need maximum length contiguous subarray that is 1122 sequence. Empty length 0 allowed. Need output max length.

Need design efficient O(N log N) or O(N).

We need find longest contiguous subarray A[l..r] (1-index inclusive) such that length even and can be partitioned into adjacent equal pairs: A[l]=A[l+1], A[l+2]=A[l+3], ..., A[r-1]=A[r]. And pair values all distinct.

Equivalently consider positions where adjacent equal pairs occur: "blocks" of length 2 at positions (i,i+1) with A_i=A_{i+1}. A subarray is 1122 iff it is a concatenation of whole adjacent equal pairs, i.e. its start l must be odd relative? Actually subarray can start at any position, but pair boundaries within subarray are l+1,l+3,... So it must be that for k=0..m-1, A_{l+2k}=A_{l+2k+1}. Thus l and l+1 are equal, l+2 and l+3 equal, etc. So it is a run of "pair starts" at positions l, l+2, l+4, ... where each pair is equal. The pair values distinct.

So if define for each position i (1-index) a "pair start" valid if A_i = A_{i+1}. Then a 1122 subarray of length 2m corresponds to a sequence of m valid pair starts at positions l, l+2, ..., l+2m-2, all valid, and their values A_l, A_{l+2}, ... distinct. Need maximize 2m.

Thus problem reduces to: Given binary valid[i] = (A_i == A_{i+1}) for i=1..N-1, and value val[i]=A_i. Need longest sequence of indices p, p+2, p+4, ..., p+2(m-1) within [1,N-1] such that valid at all these indices and val at these indices are all distinct. Length answer = 2m. Also m can be 0.

For each parity, we have a sequence of candidate pair starts along positions of that parity: indices i, i+2, i+4,... Need choose a contiguous block in this parity-indexed list such that all valid[i]=1 and values val[i] are distinct. Then m = length of block. Need max length among both parities.

Because if p parity fixed, p+2k same parity. The condition valid all positions in that selected block. If any invalid in between, cannot cross. So split into runs of consecutive valid pair starts of same parity? Wait valid indices are positions i where A_i=A_{i+1}. For same parity, indices i, i+2,... are adjacent in parity subsequence. Then within each segment, values val[i] must be distinct for chosen subsegment; need longest subsegment with all distinct values. Because if duplicate value appears, cannot include both pairs with same value. Need choose longest contiguous subarray (in parity-indexed list) within a valid segment with all distinct values. Since all positions in segment valid. Then max m = longest distinct-value subarray length in each segment. Answer 2*max_m.

def max_len_for_parity(p):
    last = {}
    left = p
    best = 0
    # positions i from p to N-2 step 2
    for i in range(p, N-1, 2):
        if A[i] != A[i+1]:
            left = i + 2
            # no need clear last; but if left > i+2? Actually if invalid at i, left = i+2. But what if left was already > i+2? Could that happen? Since processing increasing i, left <= i always? Let's check. Initially left=p <= i. After duplicate, left = previous+2 <= i. After invalid, left = i+2 > i. Then next i increases by 2, so left <= next i? If next i = i+2, left = i+2, equal. Good. So left <= current i for valid positions. If invalid at i, left = i+2, which is > current i; then next iteration current i = i+2, left equals. So okay.
            continue
        v = A[i]
        prev = last.get(v, -1)
        if prev >= left:
            left = prev + 2
        last[v] = i
        cnt = (i - left)//2 + 1
        if cnt > best: best = cnt
    return best

If duplicate, previous occurrence q is latest before current, all positions from left to q removed. Good.

Now, final code can be:

import sys

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N = int(input())
    A = list(map(int, input().split()))
    if N < 2:
        print(0)
        return
    best = 0
    for p in (0,1):
        last = {}
        left = p
        # We need iterate i from p to N-2 inclusive step 2
        for i in range(p, N-1, 2):
            if A[i] != A[i+1]:
                left = i + 2
                continue
            v = A[i]
            prev = last.get(v, -1)
            if prev >= left:
                left = prev + 2
            last[v] = i
            cur = (i - left)//2 + 1
            if cur > best:
                best = cur
    print(2*best)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

import sys
def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N = data[0]
    A = data[1:]
    best = 0
    for p in (0,1):
        last = {}
        left = p
        for i in range(p, N-1, 2):
            if A[i] != A[i+1]:
                left = i + 2
                continue
            v = A[i]
            prev = last.get(v, -1)
            if prev >= left:
                left = prev + 2
            last[v] = i
            cur = (i - left)//2 + 1
            if cur > best:
                best = cur
    print(best * 2)

Let's maybe think about a direct proof of reduction:
For a candidate subarray A[l:r] with even length 2m, define positions l+2k for k=0..m-1. The condition A[l+2k]=A[l+2k+1] means each such position is a "good pair start". Conversely, any sequence of m good pair starts with step 2 gives a valid pair partition. The distinctness condition says the values at these starts are all distinct. Therefore, for each parity, we need the longest consecutive subsequence of good starts (in that parity) with all distinct values. Our sliding window computes exactly that.

import sys

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    A = list(map(int, input().split()))
    best = 0
    for p in (0,1):
        last = {}
        left = p
        for i in range(p, N-1, 2):
            if A[i] != A[i+1]:
                left = i + 2
                continue
            v = A[i]
            prev = last.get(v, -1)
            if prev >= left:
                left = prev + 2
            last[v] = i
            cur = (i - left)//2 + 1
            if cur > best:
                best = cur
    print(best * 2)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

This assumes A line contains all N ints; input format yes. If line wrapping? AtCoder input format has A_1 ... A_N on one line, but robust reading with read split handles wrapping. Usually one line. But to be safe, use `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` to handle any whitespace. Let's use read to be robust. Memory okay. Code:

That would mean the second element of pair k is at position l+2k+1, first element of next pair is at l+2k+2, distinct positions. That would mean the second element of pair k is at position l+2k+1, first element of next pair is at l+2k+2, distinct positions. That would mean the second element of pair k is at position l+2k+1, first element of next pair is at l+2k+2, distinct positions.
